Cash still has to cover maintenance, energy, rent and scheduled principal repayments. Stopping some workloads saves variable costs but does not automatically release all contracts.

Economic life, accounting depreciation life and financing maturity must be separated. A new product launch does not scrap old equipment that day. Redeployment into batching, less latency-sensitive tasks or another customer requires validation of software compatibility, energy efficiency, memory and retrofit costs. If cash coverage after repurposing remains adequate, the assumption that "old assets rapidly go to zero" should weaken. Conversely, if renewal revenue can no longer cover maintenance and debt, declining economic value should be recognized even before accounting impairment.

### 12.4 Path Three: Refinancing Stops Cushioning the Problem, Turning Operating Stress into Payment Stress

A funding shock usually first hurts those required to pay on specified dates without sufficient collections yet in place. This is an analytical condition, not proof that every emerging compute company is riskier than a large cloud provider. Research must compare deployable cash, facilities with draw conditions already satisfied, customer prepayments and future mandatory payments, rather than rank companies solely by debt balances.

CoreWeave disclosed on August 10, 2026 that the DDTL 5.5 loan has a term of approximately five years, while the underlying customer contracts average approximately three years, indicating that some debt service depends on renewals, re-leasing or other funding sources. This does not mean revenue must vanish in years four and five, but requires renewal prices, asset residual values, customer concentration and refinancing conditions to enter the stress table together. [CoreWeave, USD 2.6 billion loan announcement, 2026-08-10](https://investors.coreweave.com/news/news-details/2026/CoreWeave-Closes-2-6-Billion-Loan-Facility-Expanding-Financing-Flexibility-for-AI-Infrastructure/default.aspx)

Profit and cash may move in different directions under financing pressure. Extending debt maturity can reduce near-term principal payments, and equity issuance can bring cash, but interest, dilution and added restrictions change per-share value. If financing merely pays irrevocable expenditure for continued expansion, the net buffer may not improve. A large contract likewise does not guarantee drawable credit: a loan may require asset delivery, collateral coverage or customer-credit conditions.

Macroeconomic inputs cannot be read in only one direction. On October 28, the Federal Reserve voted 10 to 2 to maintain the target range at 3.75%-4.00%; the two dissenters favored a 25-basis-point increase. This is a published hold decision, no longer a pending event, and cannot be interpreted as a turn to rate cuts. The US Treasury's October 30 two-year nominal, ten-year nominal and ten-year real yields were 4.56%, 4.94% and 2.60%, down 12, 6 and 6 basis points respectively from October 26. The policy rate, Treasury yields, corporate credit spreads and outstanding fixed coupons are four distinct variables: falling Treasury yields ease the discount benchmark but do not establish improved credit access, collateral haircuts or refinancing conditions for vulnerable projects. [Federal Reserve, 2026-10-28 decision](https://www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/pressreleases/monetary20261028a.htm); [US Treasury, October 2026 nominal yields](https://home.treasury.gov/resource-center/data-chart-center/interest-rates/TextView?field_tdr_date_value_month=202610&type=daily_treasury_yield_curve) and [real yields](https://home.treasury.gov/resource-center/data-chart-center/interest-rates/TextView?field_tdr_date_value_month=202610&type=daily_treasury_real_yield_curve)

CoreWeave still completed a USD 4.2 billion convertible-bond issuance on September 22, also showing that the financing window remained usable. But funds raised and capital recovered are different questions; successful issuance cannot replace validation of equipment and customer profitability. [CoreWeave, 2026-09-22 issuance-completion filing](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1769628/000176962826000432/crwv-20260917.htm)

Confirming credit contagion requires evidence that different customers and financiers are affected in common - for example, simultaneous deterioration in borrowing terms, collateral haircuts, loan renewals or payments at several companies. A widening credit spread at one company, especially with sparse quotes or changed debt maturities, cannot automatically become a uniform default probability. Without a complete holder and guarantee chain, one cannot assert that risk stops at technology companies or that it must become a banking crisis.

### 12.5 How Risk Passes Through the Three Financial Statements

| Shock | Income statement | Balance sheet | Cash flow and funding sources | Common verification error |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Customer order cuts or price reductions | Lower revenue, gross profit or future guidance | Changes in receivables, inventories and contract assets | Lower collections and working capital tied up | Current-quarter stocking and payment-term changes do not equal bad debts |
| Delayed lease commencement or acceptance | Delayed revenue recognition and changed expense-recognition timing | More construction in progress and prepayments | Payments made before recovery begins | Construction payments do not mean assets are operating |
| Falling older-equipment rents | Lower gross profit, possibly adjusted depreciation or impairment | Pressure on asset and collateral values | Lower re-leasing receipts and demands for additional collateral | Impairment is not a same-day cash outflow, but may affect financing |
| Lower investee valuations | Revaluation gains/losses or other changes depending on accounting classification | Investment carrying values adjusted | Without a sale or contribution, no equal cash outflow is necessary | Pretax items cannot be divided directly by after-tax net income |
| Debt or equity financing | Interest and per-share metrics may change | Cash, debt or equity increases | Receipts temporarily cushion the shortfall | Filing, signing, settlement and cash receipt are different milestones |
| Backstops or guarantees triggered | Expenses or provisions may be recognized | Contingent obligations become liabilities | Cash expenditure occurs when payment conditions are met | The nominal ceiling is not a current loss |

This table is a modeling interface, not one risk label for every company. Meta's company-defined 2026Q3 free cash flow was USD −2.120B, already deducting cash asset purchases/construction and finance-lease principal; cumulative FCF for the first three quarters remained USD +11.049B. One negative quarter deserves monitoring, but subtracting CapEx from FCF again would exaggerate the gap, and legal expenses and prior-year tax items cannot all be explained as deterioration in AI operations. [Meta, 2026-10-21 Q3 results and non-GAAP reconciliation](https://investor.atmeta.com/investor-news/press-release-details/2026/Meta-Reports-Third-Quarter-2026-Results/default.aspx)

Anthropic's public S-1 dated October 26, 2026 supplies a constraint in the other direction: first-half recognized revenue of USD 16.286B and operating cash flow of USD 3.929B demonstrate actual revenue and operating collections. The same document's large net loss includes noncash financing revaluation and cannot be called an equal amount of cash burn. Long-term purchases, cancellation rights and equipment leases still require period-by-period matching; both sides must enter the model. [Anthropic, S-1, MD&A cash flows and contractual commitments on pages 171-173](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/2130357/000162828026067168/anthropicpbc-sx1.htm)

### 12.6 From Companies to Stocks to Indices: Make the Arithmetic Reconcile

Earnings changes are not share-price changes. For profitable companies using consistent measures, `share-price change factor＝EPS change factor×P/E change factor` provides a first approximation. EPS must also reflect issuance, buybacks and non-operating items; P/E cannot be applied mechanically to loss-making companies.

A pure illustration: EPS falls 20% and P/E falls 25%, giving a price factor of `0.80×0.75＝0.60`, a 40% decline, not the 45% obtained by adding the two. This expression only shows price sensitivity to given assumptions; it does not establish that those assumptions will occur. If a higher discount rate has already been used to reduce the multiple, another full "interest-rate shock decline" is not added separately.

Approximate index return can be calculated using beginning weights as `Σ w_i×r_i`, stating the observation period and whether rebalancing, dividends and corporate actions are ignored. For example, assume a group represents 30% of the index and falls 40%, while the other constituents fall 10%. The index return is `30%×(−40%)+70%×(−10%)＝−19%`. The 30% is only an illustrative input, not a verified current weight in any index. Deriving a larger decline requires explicit changes to other constituents' earnings or valuation assumptions, not adding the same wealth effect again to a price path that already contains it.

When a stock falls in a capitalization-weighted index, its weight changes with its price; this does not itself require a fully replicating fund to sell more of it. Additional selling pressure requires behavioral evidence such as net redemptions, cash settlement, derivatives hedging, leverage constraints or investor risk adjustments. Secondary-market turnover of ETF units also does not mean an equal sale of constituents by the fund. Concentration implies shared risk exposure, but index ownership alone cannot establish that "passive investing must produce a stampede."

Private assets have a different timing pattern. Financing-round valuations are not continuous transaction prices, and preferences, liquidation terms and secondary-share discounts also affect comparisons. A down round may change a listed shareholder's carrying value, but the amount, taxes and recognition timing depend on the applicable accounting treatment; a valuation reduction does not necessarily erase the same amount of profit. Conversely, a successful listing can improve price discovery and financing without automatically proving that the issue valuation is reasonable.

### 12.7 When an Industry Adjustment Becomes Macroeconomic Reinforcement

Corporate capital-expenditure cuts affect equipment, construction, energy and employment; lower stock-market wealth may also change consumption. But market-capitalization losses are not contemporaneous GDP losses, corporate revenues include intermediate inputs, and overseas-produced equipment also involves an import deduction. Without domestic value-added measures, affected sectors and fund flows, a large global CapEx number cannot be divided directly by US GDP to estimate the depth of a recession.

Further spread requires new connections: creditor losses erode capital, fund redemptions force sales of unrelated assets, falling collateral triggers additional security, or banks reduce other lending as credit worsens. These are paths to investigate, not facts this chapter has confirmed as occurring. Advertising, software and other cloud revenue at mature platforms can cushion AI-project pressure; nonbank credit, project finance and guarantee chains may also carry losses beyond the platforms. Evidence from only one side cannot determine the ceiling of systemic risk.

Sovereign investors likewise cannot uniformly be treated as capital that will never exit. Equity, fiscal procurement, loans, guarantees and policy intentions materialize differently. Strategic goals may lengthen evaluation horizons without removing budget, technology-license, construction and customer-demand constraints. Track payments made, draw conditions and project milestones; a national-project name does not replace a guarantee document.

If an adjustment occurs, its consequences need not be limited to an industry-wide contraction. Operators with weaker financing may sell assets; companies with stronger cash and customer resources may consolidate capacity; lower compute prices may increase application adoption. The same change can benefit consumers while imposing losses on older-asset financiers. This does not allow winners to be named in advance, because acquisition prices, retrofit costs, contractual burdens and competition still determine cash returns.

Regional divergence also requires item-by-item argument. Customer budgets, technology access, energy costs and policy support across economies may cause adjustments to occur at different times. These differences neither guarantee a natural advantage for one region nor insulate its stock market from global funding changes. Index constituents may change with competition, but that is not the same conclusion as "the logic of index investing has now failed," which requires independent evidence on diversification, costs and long-term returns.

### 12.8 Leading, Coincident and Lagging: Update on Evidence, Not a Scenario's Timetable

| Observation layer | Changes that may appear earlier | Subsequent validation | Lag that should not be assumed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Customer operations | Trial-to-paid conversion, renewal discounts and customer-budget changes | Revenue recognition, subsequent collections and cash gross profit | Every customer cuts orders in the same quarter |
| Asset delivery | Construction milestones, power connections and equipment acceptance | Lease commencement, billable utilization and operating/maintenance costs | Announced operations mean immediate full load |
| Financing terms | New loan quotes, collateral requirements and covenant adjustments | Actual draws, maturity renewals and payments | CDS leads stock prices by a fixed number of months |
| Financial reporting | Changes in payment terms, capitalization and estimates | Impairment, profit/loss recognition and cash payments | The accounting recognition date is the start of economic loss |
| Market pricing | Changes in expectations and risk premiums | Whether orders, cash and financing follow | An earlier share-price fall proves the fundamental forecast correct |

For each event, record at least the original expectation, actual result, parameter changed and disconfirming evidence; do not automatically declare a systemic crisis on "any two signals." A prediction that fails to occur within its original window is scored under its original conditions. Later new risks need a new argument; the clock cannot be reset.

As of October 31, 2026, the latest cloud-provider results make "divergence between demand growth and cash recovery" a more direct observation: Microsoft still has a positive cash surplus, Amazon's trailing-twelve-month free-cash-flow shortfall has widened, and Alphabet also requires operating performance to be separated from investment revaluation and construction expenditure. Specific figures appear in the profit chapter. These findings raise the priority of testing funding coverage, but do not independently establish a collapse in industry-wide end demand or a general financing interruption. The next check is whether sustainable operations and secured funding can cover the gaps, and whether pressure expands into common orders, credit and payments across multiple companies. Without that evidence chain, localized pressure is not described as the arrival of a systemic crisis.

## Chapter 13: Delay and Absorption - What Does the Extra Time Actually Change?

A project entering service six months late, a model company listing a year late, and a highly valued industry rising for several more quarters can all be called "delay," but they do not share the same cash consequences. Risk depends on who keeps paying during the extra time, who receives cash later, whether assets remain useful, and whether new demand adequately compensates for waiting. Delay can widen a shortfall or allow prematurely built capacity to be absorbed; research must permit both outcomes.

### 13.1 Four Delays, Four Different Cash Statements

**Engineering delay** occurs between land, equipment, power supply, construction and acceptance. If equipment has been paid for, debt is accruing interest and customers have not begun leasing, waiting ties up more capital. If expansion not yet ordered can be postponed, or customer prepayments cover part of the expenditure, cash pressure may be smaller. Engineering delay does not necessarily mean declining customer demand, still less justify treating all planned MW as fully invested.

**Customer-deployment delay** occurs between trials and formal procurement. A trial's value does not mean procurement has assigned a long-term budget. Even when model capabilities meet requirements, permissions, data, reliability, liability and migration from existing systems may remain obstacles. No demand, unproven demand, and demand that exists but is difficult to deliver must be distinguished, as each implies different revenue and expense assumptions.

**Technology-maturity delay** affects the range of billable tasks. If capability improves more slowly than the investment plan assumes, continued training, hiring and iteration consume funds. But later maturity may also allow companies to adopt cheaper hardware and more efficient architectures, avoiding premature technological lock-in. Demonstration results, public benchmarks and production acceptance must be recorded separately; a model's release date is not the industry's cash-recovery date.

**Financing delay** first changes liquidity. Uncompleted financing or an IPO cannot pay today's bills. Even if financing is completed later, its net proceeds, use restrictions, interest and dilution matter. If the project itself has positive net present value, matching maturities may solve the problem. If it still cannot pay back under reasonable cost and demand assumptions, merely extending repayment postpones the loss rather than absorbing it.

All four delays can occur together, but each cannot repeatedly add the same equipment expenditure. The model should retain the original and latest plans, move the dates of each payment, acceptance and revenue commencement separately, then examine the cash trough and remaining return.

### 13.2 How Delay Amplifies Risk: Time Itself Is Not the Problem

The most direct mechanism is that receipts are late while expenditure is not. Equipment and construction have already been paid for, the project must still pay for storage, maintenance, rent or essential staff, and it may face competition from a new equipment generation by the time leasing begins. Even if ultimate nominal revenue is unchanged, discounted cash flow value falls. Operating losses increase further if customers demand discounts or cancel as a result.

The second mechanism is continued growth in commitments. To maintain scale targets, companies lock in the next batch of equipment, electricity and data centers before existing projects have demonstrated returns. Future noncancelable payments grow faster than available funding and end-customer collections. The real risk variables are new net obligations and their revocability, not the total investment announced in the news. Where contracts contain cancellation rights, substitute purchases, deposits and cross-guarantees, duplication must be removed transaction by transaction.

The third mechanism links asset values and financing. Falling rental prices for older equipment reduce collateral value; lenders demand more equity, and projects issue more shares to continue building. Even successful financing may impose greater dilution on existing shareholders. If the guarantor is also the equipment supplier, a sale previously considered complete may leave a future credit obligation. Whether this forms a loop depends on guarantee triggers and customer payments; an investment-relationship diagram alone cannot establish fictitious revenue.

The fourth mechanism is attractive accounting profit obscuring a longer cash payback period. Extending depreciation lives changes the timing of expenses, not the equipment price already paid. Investment revaluations may lift net income without bringing operating cash. This mechanism requires checking asset classes and accounting estimates. Long depreciation lives alone do not establish fraud, and the end of depreciation lives cannot be presented as a uniform crisis certain to erupt in a particular year.

If these conditions have not appeared, declaring that "risk has accumulated further" merely because the market has not yet fallen makes the judgment impossible to falsify.

### 13.3 Delay Can Also Help Absorption: Loss-Bearing and Additional Options Are Key

If expansion remains cancelable, companies can slow construction while waiting for proof of customer payment, reducing a concentrated near-term supply release. Utilization of existing assets may still rise even if capital-expenditure growth falls. This is an absorption path that does not first require a collapse in end demand. The focus is cancellation cost, existing customer commitments and investment needed to remain competitive, rather than defining every CapEx reduction as bad news.

Technological efficiency can also change recovery from the same assets. If model routing, caching, quantization or task allocation enables the same hardware to serve more paid tasks, older equipment may generate revenue for longer. If competitive price cuts capture most efficiency gains, customers benefit while operators' cash returns may not improve. Only by combining usage, effective pricing and unit cost can one determine whether efficiency is absorbing investment or rendering old assets obsolete.

Gradually maturing customer payments are among the strongest forms of disconfirming evidence. Observe actual renewals, deployment scale and cash contributions from the same customer cohort, rather than counting new financing, free credits and short promotions as sustained demand. If model providers' incremental revenue comes from customers' operating budgets, cloud-provider investment recovery has a source distinct from shareholder injections. Supplier financing can be a bridge rather than a perpetual source of demand; that possibility should remain in the model.

Long-term capital may also bridge the construction period. If its duration covers engineering and operating ramp-up, existing investment has a chance to turn into billable service. But longer-term does not mean cheaper or less conditional. Sovereign capital, policy support and internal corporate cash all require verification of actual contributions, risk-sharing and exit terms. Strategic positioning in an announcement indicates intent only; it cannot be taken to mean all losses will be absorbed.

### 13.4 A Reproducible Model of Waiting

The following illustration uses monetary units and no real company's forecasts. Assume 100 is invested today, with the original plan receiving net cash of 60 at each of the first and second year-ends, no terminal value, and a 10% discount rate. Net cash already includes operating costs and tax; the project measure excludes financing interest.

| Scenario | Cash timing | NPV | Explanation |
|---|---|---:|---|
| Original plan | Pay 100 at t=0; receive 60 at each of t=1, 2 | 4.13 | `−100+60/1.1+60/1.1²` |
| Only collections are one year late | Pay 100 at t=0; receive 60 at each of t=2, 3 | −5.33 | Revenue is unchanged; waiting reduces value by 9.47 |
| Expenditure and collections all move one year later | Pay 100 at t=1; receive 60 at each of t=2, 3 | 3.76 | The whole project is delayed; one cannot move only revenue to manufacture a loss |
| Waiting reduces equipment investment to 90 | Pay 90 at t=1; receive 60 at each of t=2, 3 | 12.85 | Merely illustrates a procurement-price reduction sufficient to compensate for waiting; not a claim that reality will follow this path |

The table also reminds us that cash needs during delay and value loss are not the same number. In the first delayed scenario, a company may still wait comfortably because it has no debt falling due. The third scenario has positive net present value yet may face liquidity problems if expenditure precedes financing receipts. A project-return model and a debt-service schedule should coexist.

If maintenance expenditure during waiting is added, deduct it on the dates incurred. If borrowing interest has already been deducted in shareholder cash flow, do not count the same financing burden again. Real projects should also include leases, tax shields, default compensation and residual value; this example's 10% discount rate cannot replace every company's cost of capital.

### 13.5 How Current Project Evidence Constrains the Delay Narrative

Akamai disclosed a seven-year USD 11.6B agreement with Anthropic in September 2026 and expected related capital expenditure of USD 5.5B, illustrating the need to match construction expenditure and revenue across periods. Subtracting 5.5 from 11.6 does not produce project profit, because contractual service costs, payment and collection timing, taxes and discounting have not entered the calculation. A large contract can support construction while also requiring upfront investment to fulfill it. [Akamai, related 8-K and agreement announcement, 2026-09](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1086222/000119312526401048/d288154d8k.htm)

APLD disclosed on October 7, 2026 that an additional 75MW had become ready for service on October 1, a delivery advance that cannot be ignored. Yet its HPC revenue for FY27Q1 ended August 31 still included fit-out and cost recoveries. Operating capacity is not billable utilization, still less stable full-year rent already earned. Engineering milestones, lease-commencement structure and group cash should be kept in three separate records. [Applied Digital, FY27Q1 results, 2026-10-07](https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/10/07/3376854/0/en/applied-digital-reports-fiscal-first-quarter-2027-results.html)

Oracle and STACK's September 30, 2026 statement on Jupiter said flooding had not damaged critical facilities and the project remained on schedule; on October 23, Oracle further disclosed construction contracts and payment progress. Both are engineering information from parties to the project, not independent acceptance. Equally, flood headlines alone do not justify declaring inevitable delay. Until equipment commissioning, power testing and customer-acceptance materials are obtained, payment and GPUs entering production remain different states. [Oracle and STACK, 2026-09-30 statement](https://www.oracle.com/news/announcement/project-jupiter-statement-on-southern-new-mexico-floods-2026-09-30/); [Oracle, 2026-10-23 construction progress](https://www.oracle.com/news/announcement/project-jupiter-advances-new-mexico-workforce-growth-and-awards-nearly-1-billion-in-contracts-to-local-businesses-2026-10-23/)

Minimum purchases, contracts with cancellation rights and equipment leases in Anthropic's public S-1 also cannot be combined into homogeneous debt due that day. They meet revenue and financing in different years. The public filing improves research visibility, but does not complete issue pricing, settlement or receipt of proceeds. [Anthropic, 2026-10-26 S-1, contractual commitments and financing sections](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/2130357/000162828026067168/anthropicpbc-sx1.htm)

The common significance of these cases is to turn "is delay dangerous?" into a verifiable question, not to prove that the entire industry follows one calendar. Strong supplier financials and improving delivery at some projects can coexist with cash tied up in others.

### 13.6 Why Electricity, Policy and Technology Narratives Change the Length of the Wait

Power constraints may slow new supply, protecting near-term utilization at already energized capacity while increasing cash tied up in projects not yet operating. National electricity-demand forecasts cannot distinguish these two types of participant; analysis must reach the project's grid, permits, connection equipment, backup and electricity contracts. More distant nuclear or fusion plans may affect long-term energy choices but cannot replace today's available power in advance. Power agreements, construction financing, commercial operation and reliable supply require separate validation.

Monetary policy also works in both directions. Lower financing costs may raise project net present value and ease near-term debt pressure, while encouraging further investment in assets whose returns remain unproven. Higher rates raise financing hurdles, but by restraining inefficient new construction may also help existing assets absorb supply. Policy effects depend on corporate responses; one cannot assume "easing must inflate the bubble, tightening must trigger a crash."

Changes in technology narratives are even easier to misread. Greater capability, new tasks and expansion into robotics or other physical applications may indeed invalidate an initially conservative demand model. Yet reliability, cost, regulation and delivery still stand between a capability demonstration and a customer contract. Conversely, researchers cannot treat every new product as a distraction: if renewals and cash prove additional uses, the original market boundary should expand. The AGI and monetization chapters examine specific technological evidence; this chapter does not convert release counts into probability scores.

### 13.7 Constraints from History, Not "A Later Burst Must Mean a Bigger Fall"

Historical market peaks and troughs are observable, but "how little prices would have fallen without the rescue" cannot be read directly from actual prices. An amplification multiple using such an unidentified counterfactual decline as its denominator is not a statistical law. Debt, banking systems, demographics, industries and policy conditions across crises cannot be condensed into a uniform number of years of delay either.

Likewise, a price index returning to its old high does not mean every investor has recovered their investment. Inclusion of dividends in total return, initial purchase date, exchange rates, fees, taxes and inflation all change the outcome. An index returning to a high after changing constituents does not mean every company from that time has recovered. Historical recovery periods can warn against matching long-term assets with short-term funding needs, but cannot designate when to buy the dip in this cycle or when payback is certain.

### 13.8 Use a Project Table to Judge Absorption Versus Continued Accumulation

| Project field | Direction closer to absorption | Direction closer to accumulation | Treatment when missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Payments made and future mandatory payments | Uncommitted expenditure is adjustable; cancellation costs are manageable | New noncancelable obligations outgrow funding sources | Do not substitute headline total investment |
| Engineering and lease commencement | Electricity connection, acceptance and paid lease commencement are progressively completed | Repeated delays while equipment and maintenance payments continue | Mark as not yet accepted; do not record plans as achievements |
| End customers | Renewals and paid expansion generate cash | Collections depend on another financing round or greater subsidies | Do not equate ARR with cash receipts |
| Asset uses | Repurposed older equipment has real customers and net cash | Renewal discounts, retrofit costs and power consumption erode value | Retain a residual-value range; do not impose a uniform retirement age |
| Financing maturity | Payments and debt service fall after a visible recovery period | Near-term maturities and narrowing collateral headroom | Distinguish signed facilities from drawable amounts |
| Shareholder returns | Cash improvement is not offset by maintenance investment and dilution | The project survives while per-share value is continually diluted | Do not substitute "not bankrupt" for investment success |

To judge the direction of delay, compare net changes in these fields. If delay accompanies a narrowing funding gap, improving end-customer payments and greater discretionary spending, absorption should be acknowledged. Only if it accompanies growing irrevocable obligations, financing dependence and economic obsolescence is there reason to conclude that risk has increased. If data for both directions remain missing, the conclusion is that no determination can yet be made; repeating a more distant year cannot supply the missing evidence.

## Appendix A: Research Framework and Methodology

### A Framework for Studying the AI Industry Cycle: Financing Links, Physical Delivery and Capital Recovery

This framework tracks a sequence of conversions that must happen on time, rather than an ever-larger CapEx check: can financing buy equipment, can equipment receive power and be delivered, can delivery generate paid use, and can paid use leave sufficient cash? A slowdown anywhere can disconnect earlier orders/revenue from later capital returns. Conversely, pressure at one link does not directly prove that end demand across the industry has disappeared.

This appendix retains the analytical framework and dated examples from the 27 October 2026 edition. New company earnings, financing and macro inputs on 28 - 30 October appear in this edition's earnings, financing, valuation and overall-conclusions chapters; old examples do not automatically become 31 October current values. Historical cases test mechanisms, retain original years and do not serve as current industry statistics. This is a research tool, not a frequency-calibrated “AI crash probability model.”

### I. Research Methodology

#### Label Evidence Sources Separately from the Nature of Claims

One company document may contain recognized revenue, unpaid contracts, future guidance and risk warnings. Coming from a securities-regulatory system does not turn all four into realized facts. Each core figure needs two labels: source traceability, and whether the claim is fact, forecast, assumption or judgment.

Annualized revenue per megawatt for selected short contracts cannot be multiplied by all committed power: contract duration, commissioning rates and customer prices differ. Convertible principal, proceeds after discounts and available cash after hedging costs are likewise not interchangeable. Establish numerators, denominators and cash classifications before arithmetic, avoiding precise but meaningless totals.

| Nature of claim | Original information to preserve | What it cannot replace |
|---|---|---|
| Realized fact | Reporting period, accounting basis, currency, notes or announcement terms | One quarter cannot directly become a future full year |
| Company plan/guidance | Release date, applicable period, scope, conditions and later revisions | Not payments or deliveries already made |
| External forecast | Institution, model sample, period, scenario and key assumptions | Not company commitments or market facts |
| Author judgment | Supporting facts, derivation, counterevidence and missing variables | Source count cannot substitute for valid reasoning |

Original contracts, regulatory filings, official earnings and statistical-agency materials take priority for terms and realized data; media and research institutions provide event leads, expert interpretation and forecast references. Traceable primary sources are preferred, but disclosure may still not reveal ultimate payers, customer gross profit or project payback; these gaps must remain explicit.

Cross-institution reconciliation requires more than “all being three-star evidence”: entity, period, currency, cash/noncash classification, gross/net basis and transaction boundaries must align. Three institutions may use the same company guidance; three forecasts citing shared information are not three independent pieces of evidence.

#### Separate Financial Substance before Aggregating Exposure

| Type | Terms to verify | Ledger treatment |
|---|---|---|
| Equity and loans | Actual payment, staged closing, undrawn balances, maturity and recovery conditions | Separate paid assets from unpaid commitments |
| Equipment/cloud purchases | Minimum purchases, cancellation rights, acceptance and settlement progress | Separate contractual obligations from actual procurement/payments |
| Prepayments | Refundability, credit against future bills and recipient-use restrictions | Cash has arrived, but revenue is not necessarily recognized |
| Residual guarantees/credit support | Triggers, maximum liability, duration, indemnification rights and collateral | Contingent exposure, not current cash spending |
| Warrants | Vesting/exercise, fair value, accounting classification and potential dilution | Review with procurement incentives and statement effects |

Equity may arrive in installments over years, while cloud contracts may span hardware replacement cycles. Buyer CapEx and seller equipment revenue are two sides of one transaction. Adding ultimate customers' application payments requires first drawing industry boundaries; otherwise money passing through three links becomes three falsely independent demands.

### II. Financing Networks: From “Large Numbers” to Cash Dependence and Contingent Obligations

#### Linked Structures Do Not Mean False Transactions

A supplier investing in a customer that buys its equipment first establishes intertwined financing/business relationships. This may reduce procurement uncertainty and accelerate real capacity, or make sales growth dependent on continuing finance. Reciprocal contracts, similar amounts and overlapping identities justify examination, but do not prove investment fully recirculated, much less fraud.

Distinguish three layers: disclosed relationships, individually reconcilable funds flows, and who bears loss after customer default. Only linking payments, collections, contract performance and ultimate external-customer revenue establishes whether end cash can sustain the network.

#### Two Verified Cases: Guarantees and Warrants Are Not Cash Investment

NVIDIA's 17 August 2026 8-K discloses that OpenAI is the tenant at SB Energy's Portsmouth project. The initial residual-value guarantee concerns approximately 4.25GW of IT load, with cumulative payment liability capped at USD 105 billion, conditional on service readiness and other requirements expected to be met from 2028. It covers the difference between an agreed minimum value after default and recoveries through replacement leasing or sale, with indemnification and termination terms. It is therefore a long-term contingent obligation to monitor, not USD 105 billion NVIDIA has already invested or an equal expected loss. [NVIDIA 2026-08-17 8-K](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1045810/000104581026000069/nvda-20260817.htm)

AMD's OpenAI arrangement combines procurement with equity incentives. The October 2025 disclosure of warrants for up to 160 million shares at USD 0.01 each includes staged vesting tied to procurement milestones, share prices and technical/commercial conditions. The explicit initial binding purchase commitment is 1GW; full vesting requires corresponding purchases up to 6GW and other conditions. The full ultimate target cannot be treated as orders already delivered. [AMD 2025-10-06 8-K](https://ir.amd.com/financial-information/sec-filings/content/0001193125-25-230895/d28189d8k.htm)

Accounting also goes beyond “no cash, only future dilution.” AMD's 2025 third-quarter filing says grant-date warrant fair value reduces related revenue as recognized; fair-value changes while liability-classified enter other income/expense. Its 2026 second-quarter filing says the OpenAI and Meta warrants, each up to 160 million shares, were unvested/unexercisable at 27 June, with no quarterly/half-year statement effect, and were classified as liabilities pending satisfaction of the equity-classification conditions. The first describes treatment, the second period-end status; neither means “no revenue effect forever.” [AMD 2025Q3 10-Q, note 15](https://ir.amd.com/financial-information/sec-filings/content/0000002488-25-000166/amd-20250927.htm); [AMD 2026Q2 10-Q, Warrants](https://ir.amd.com/financial-information/sec-filings/content/0000002488-26-000123/amd-20260627.htm)

These cases require two additional model schedules: quarterly contractual cash flows, and payments/equity changes contingent on specified conditions. “No current effect” cannot exclude future risk, while “large future ceilings” cannot prove present losses.

#### Three Risk Concentrations

**First, does customer purchasing power depend on the next funding round?** Track nonfinancing customer collections, operating consumption, available cash, committed facilities and procurement/debt payments due over the next twelve months. New funding eases liquidity but cannot replace better customer unit economics. If payments depend on continued shareholder contributions, record that dependence rather than treat all new orders as mature end demand.

**Second, can equipment/projects become billable capacity within financing maturities?** Equipment payments, construction, grid connection, acceptance and lease commencement may fall to different companies. Delays need not cancel final demand but prolong cash-flow gaps. Recoverable values differ across GPUs, cooling, land, power-connection rights and leases; one depreciation life cannot represent them all.

**Third, does default return risk upstream?** Offtake, guarantees, prepayments, repurchase or indemnification clauses may transmit customer credit risk to equipment suppliers. Stress analysis needs default triggers, replacement leasing, collateral recovery and realizability of indemnification, distinguishing nominal contractual ceilings, recognized accounting amounts and scenario losses.

For a purely illustrative shortfall guarantee, let guaranteed value be G, realizable net post-default recovery R and liability ceiling L. Conditional payment can initially be estimated as `min(L, max(0, G − R))`; real contracts also require amounts already paid, performance conditions and termination terms. Multiplying the ceiling by an uncalibrated probability cannot produce a credible precise industry-loss forecast.

#### What to Retain from Telecom-Equipment Financing Comparisons

The comparison should concern supplier financing, customer credit and equipment residual value. Financing numerators must distinguish loans, equity, offtake and guarantees; revenue denominators must match entities/periods. Without a consistent sample, scale multiples do not measure crisis severity.

Compare who pays customers, whether revenue requires delivery acceptance, whether upstream parties backstop losses, debt maturities, equipment resale and how many new projects core cash can support. Mature cloud-business cash and emerging compute operators' financing conditions differ substantially and require separate layers of analysis. The weakest participant's financing pressure does not establish simultaneous industry-wide default.

### III. The Physical Supply Chain: Deliverable Capacity Explains Revenue Timing

#### HBM and Packaging: Different Denominators Are Not Interchangeable

High-bandwidth memory (HBM) capacity per unit, bandwidth, yield and supply terms affect accelerator costs/delivery. But HBM's share of a GPU bill of materials, server purchase-price composition and cloud providers' total CapEx are three different questions. Public data cannot reconstruct uniform industry-wide bills of materials and pure-AI CapEx denominators, so this framework supplies no deceptively precise industry-wide HBM percentage.

Retain the following cost-transmission model without predetermining who earns the profits:

`Server procurement cost = accelerators and associated boards + CPU/memory + networking + racks and other components`.

`Project capital expenditure = server/network procurement + facility, electrical and cooling construction + other project costs requiring capitalization`.

If accelerator prices already include HBM/advanced packaging, memory and packaging vendors' sales cannot be added again to project investment. Assessing HBM providers' profit expansion requires product mix, bargaining mechanisms, unit costs and new capacity, not only revenue or company-wide gross margin. Physical bottlenecks can redistribute supply-chain profits but do not themselves prove circular financing.

#### Power Constraints: Units and Geographic Boundaries

Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory's June 2026 United States Data Center Energy Usage Report: 2025 Update gives a 2030 reference scenario of 649TWh annual consumption, approximately 11.8% of US electricity, and a combined uncertainty range of 521-843TWh, or 9.5%-15.3%. This forecasts multiple data-center types, not actual pure-AI consumption or contracted power demand. The reference case depends on equipment shipments, operations, cooling and other inputs; “2025” in the title does not mean the projected endpoint has already occurred. [LBNL official report page, June 2026](https://datacenters.lbl.gov/publications/united-states-data-center-energy-2025)

TWh is energy over a period; GW is power. Dividing 649TWh by 8760 annual hours gives average load of approximately 74.1GW, still not facility nameplate power, IT capacity or grid-connectable capacity. Peaks/troughs, utilization, cooling and local reliability affect capacity needs; differently defined power figures cannot simply be subtracted into a national shortage.

The US forecast and IEA's global 2030 central projection of 950TWh differ in institution, release date and assumptions and lack reconciled regional components. Dividing 649 by 950 does not directly establish US share or combine them into a regional table. The former informs US sensitivities, the latter global direction; differences remain model-reconciliation items. [IEA April 2026 forecast](https://www.iea.org/reports/key-questions-on-energy-and-ai/executive-summary)

National totals indicate potentially large demand; timely project delivery depends on the local grid. Five project dates are more explanatory: secured power agreement, key-equipment delivery, construction completion, grid-connection acceptance and customer billing commencement. Behind-the-meter (BTM) generation, storage or cross-region dispatch may help, but involve fuel, emissions permits, backup and reliability. All standby gas-turbine capacity is not year-round supply capacity.

Higher power prices need not pass one-for-one into API prices. Operators may absorb them through efficiency, routing and product mix, or lower margins. Durable pass-through requires demand and bargaining power. Examine equal-quality task charges, energy per task and delivery costs together.

#### Advanced Packaging: From “Industry-Wide Queues” to Technology-Specific Delivery

CoWoS and other advanced packaging remain important, but technology type, capacity definition, actual output, yields, customer qualification and product size need separate records. Wafer counts, package counts and deliverable accelerators are different units. Larger products may consume more process resources, and monthly nominal capacity growth need not produce proportionate deliveries.

Capacity reservations, delivery waits and order-taking suspension are different statuses. Without original order rules, applicable products and lines, one customer's schedule cannot establish an industry-wide stop in orders; third-party capacity forecasts are not TSMC realized output.

For each affected company, ask four questions: which packaging is secured, where the delivery bottleneck lies, which quarter's revenue delay affects, and whether alternative processes or acceptance conditions exist. Pre-delivery inventory building differs from unsold finished goods; customer/supplier prepayments and equipment arrivals help establish actual execution.

### IV. Three-Layer CapEx Analysis and Cash-Recovery Models

#### Three Layers and Deduplication

| Layer | Assets studied | Duplication to remove | Cash-recovery clues |
|---|---|---|---|
| Compute/networking | Accelerators, servers, networking and supporting storage | HBM/packaging already included in chip prices are not added again | Billable utilization, effective prices, unit costs and replacement frequency |
| Physical data-center facilities | Buildings, electrical systems, cooling, racks and others | Leased assets and the lessor's same construction expenditure are not counted twice | Powered capacity, lease commencement, occupancy and maintenance CapEx |
| External energy/interconnection | Generation, transmission/distribution, connection and related assets | Separate cloud CapEx from utility investment by entity; PPAs are not entirely CapEx | Commissioning, power contracts, capital-return arrangements and cost bearers |

The global investment total remains unverified. Even sourced institutional forecasts require aligned windows, countries, power/construction inclusion, cash purchases and leased-asset recognition before comparison. Without original reports and breakdowns, retain “unverified”; three institutions agreeing directionally do not supply amount evidence.

Bottom-up calculations build bridges from each company's cash-flow statement, lease notes, guidance and project announcements. Cash asset purchases inform cash financing needs, while new finance-lease assets inform economic investment. If timing differs, avoid adding initial recognition again to future principal payments. Where company “CapEx” definitions differ, display differences before offering comparable subsets.

#### TAM Is Demand Potential, Not Cash Belonging to Investors

Total addressable market (TAM) is potential annual demand under price, user and penetration assumptions, neither actual recognized revenue nor FCF obtainable by equipment purchasers. Application payments may fund cloud, model APIs and equipment; cross-chain revenue cannot be repeatedly summed to cover one layer's capital investment.

Different ramps toward the same terminal annual-revenue target produce different cumulative revenue. Below, terminal annual revenue is 100 currency units, illustrating timing only, not AI market size.

| Assumed revenue path | Five-year cumulative revenue | Example with terminal annual revenue 100 |
|---|---|---|
| Terminal annual revenue R_T achieved from the outset in every year | `5 × R_T` | 500 |
| Continuous linear rise from zero to R_T over five years | `2.5 × R_T` | 250 |
| Five annual values at 20%, 40%, 60%, 80%, 100% of terminal value | `3 × R_T` | 300 |

If initial annual revenue is nonzero, include it explicitly. The continuous-linear general formula is `Cumulative revenue = duration × (initial annualized revenue + terminal annualized revenue) / 2`; discrete annual records must be summed year by year, without mixing continuous areas and year-end values.

#### Returns Must Be Calculated from Periodic Cash Flows

Revenue divided by cumulative CapEx is at most a rough ratio of two same-currency amounts. It deducts no operating costs, taxes or reinvestment and contains no time value. It cannot be named a capital-recovery multiple or establish a negative internal rate of return (IRR). A full model uses one company/project boundary and records genuinely attributable operating earnings/investment each period.

For enterprise free cash flow, one can use:

`FCFF_t = EBIT_t × (1 − tax_rate_t) + depreciation_and_amortization_t − capital_expenditure_t − increase_in_working_capital_t`.

Tax requires separate modeling with losses, deferred tax or tax shields, not mechanically applying a positive-profit rate. Depreciation is deducted in operating income then added back as noncash; after deducting CapEx, it cannot become a second cash investment deduction. FCFF does not additionally deduct financing interest; financing costs enter the matching discount rate. Switching to equity cash flow requires a separate debt/interest schedule.

Net present value (NPV) is `Σ CF_t / (1+k)^t`, including initial investment and later cash flows; IRR is the rate making NPV zero. With multiple cash-flow sign changes, IRR may be nonunique or nonexistent; prioritize NPV at a specified cost of capital. Remaining usable assets/operations can have terminal value, but do not count going-concern terminal value and the same assets' disposal proceeds twice.

Pure illustration: initial investment 100, followed by five year-end net project inflows of 25, with no residual value. Undiscounted inflows total 125, or 1.25 times initial investment; at 10% capital cost, NPV is approximately −5.23 and IRR approximately 7.93%. Even “cumulative cash exceeds investment” does not establish earning the cost of capital, much less returns inferred only from cumulative revenue.

If the initial investment is still paid today but all five payments of 25 are delayed one year, assuming no changes in revenue, costs, residual value or default, NPV falls further to approximately −13.85. Undiscounted cumulative revenue/cash is unchanged, yet value falls approximately 8.62; additional holding costs from delays must be deducted separately. The difference arises from collection timing, requires no assumption of zero end demand, and is not a return estimate for real AI projects.

#### Assess Cash Gaps, RPO and Depreciation Separately

CapEx/operating cash flow (CapEx/OCF), for the same period and positive OCF, measures pressure on operating-cash investment coverage, not default probability. If OCF is negative or near zero, show the cash gap directly rather than let a huge ratio obscure scale. Comparisons should also list cash, upcoming debt maturities, CapEx, dividends/buybacks and secured financing.

`Simplified ending available cash = beginning available cash + OCF − cash capital expenditure − maturing debt principal − dividends/buybacks + secured new financing + other net cash flows`.

“Secured” requires distinguishing signed facilities from actual drawdown conditions. Interest already included in OCF is not deducted again; finance-lease principal outside cash CapEx belongs in separate financing outflows. Unrestricted and restricted cash cannot offset near-term payments equally.

Remaining performance obligations (RPO) mainly represent contracts awaiting revenue recognition, not immediately usable cash. RPO divided by annual OCF has a years dimension; using quarterly OCF requires stating annualization. This appendix has no verified universal automatic-downgrade threshold. Ratings require company-specific duration, cash coverage and methodology; a ratio's threshold cannot be transferred to another metric.

More useful RPO analysis examines recognition timing, performance costs, prepayments, cancellation/renewal and counterparty credit. Depreciation analysis examines lives, replacement speed, impairment and maintenance. CapEx near depreciation proves neither peak construction nor compulsory external financing; it is one observation requiring asset/cash-flow context.

### V. Using the Framework and Recognizing Its Limits

#### Preserve Three Possibilities in One Monitoring Table

| Path to test | Evidence needed together | Signal insufficient alone |
|---|---|---|
| Investment gradually absorbed | Better billable utilization, retention and cash gross profit; falling financing dependence | CapEx continuing to increase alone |
| Demand exists but returns are pressured | Rising use, lower unit charges/effective margins, continuing replacement investment | API cuts or token growth alone |
| Credit and supply contract | Payment/lease delays, deteriorating funding, cancellations or firm-spending cuts corroborate one another | One trading day's share-price decline |

Each update should answer whether new facts alter revenue, costs, timing or financing structure; which parameter should change; and which counterevidence condition was triggered. This avoids selecting only the side consistent with prior beliefs when strong earnings coexist with financing risk.

Evidence tables should also separate “reporting period” and “last verification date.” Rereading a June report in September improves traceability, not turns it into September actuals. Without a financing MOU status update, retain the last verified stage rather than assume closing. Record factual status, model-parameter changes and scenario-weight changes separately, preventing an old announcement newly obtained from becoming another fresh forecast success.

#### Scope and Missing Evidence

The framework suits comparisons of project-recovery paths, earnings sensitivity to demand/prices and participants' cash/maturity mismatches. Public information alone cannot fully identify private-company unit costs, ultimate customer budgets, cancellation rights or bank-credit constraints.

US/Chinese ecosystems differ in financing, product restrictions, energy allocation and customers; one set of ratios cannot transfer directly. Government support, sovereign capital and strategic long-term procurement may change financing duration, but each must be identified as fiscal expenditure, equity, loan, guarantee or policy intention. “National projects” do not automatically receive unlimited backstops.

Still needed are deduplicated transaction funding ledgers, comparable customer cash gross margins, quarterly project lease-commencement/equipment-replacement data, and same-time/same-maturity credit spreads. Without them, offer conditional risk judgments, not one industry recovery rate, crisis date or trading instruction.

## Appendix B: Cross-Border Channels and Instrument Boundaries

A judgment about the AI capital cycle cannot be converted directly into a personal trading list. Even if an investor correctly judges the direction of earnings and valuations, losses may still result from an inapplicable funding channel, mismatched contract maturity, a narrowing premium, inadequate margin or differences in product structure. This chapter retains V1.7's explanation of instrument mechanics. The legal-information verification date remains October 27, 2026; neither V1.8 nor this V1.9 reverified every jurisdiction, broker rule and product contract. Specific applicability is subject to the rules and contracts in force at the relevant time, and this chapter does not replace licensed institutions' reviews of identity, source of funds and product suitability.

### I. Channels Before Products: Four Arrangements Are Not One Offshore Account

Any discussion of cross-border investment must answer at least four separate questions: who may participate, how funds enter and exit, what assets may be held, and who bears distribution and custody responsibilities. A product's listing in Hong Kong or the United States answers only the listing-venue question. It does not establish that an individual in mainland China can buy it through any account, still less that the purpose of personal foreign-exchange purchases has been authorized.

| Arrangement | Relationship between funds and assets | Boundary this chapter can establish |
|---|---|---|
| Southbound Cross-boundary Wealth Management Connect | Eligible participants invest in eligible products through paired dedicated accounts, with a closed-loop flow of funds | Banks and securities firms have their respective participation and distribution arrangements; this is not a general Hong Kong/US stock account |
| Southbound Stock Connect | Trading within the mutual market-access mechanism and the applicable eligible list | Listing on HKEX does not equal inclusion in Southbound Stock Connect; ETFs also have specific eligibility requirements |
| QDII funds and similar products | Qualified domestic institutions raise, custody and manage offshore investment portfolios | Individuals hold fund units; this does not confer eligibility to remit funds independently to any offshore account |
| General offshore securities accounts and services | Also involves the account-opening jurisdiction, location of service provision, client identity and source of funds | The existence of an account, access to software or full offshore licensing is not independently sufficient to establish that the relevant services offered into mainland China and funding route are compliant |

The rectification arrangements for illegal cross-border securities, futures and fund operations announced on May 22, 2026 target offshore institutions and assisting parties conducting relevant business in mainland China without approval, and include requirements to clear illegal existing business. Their scope cannot be expanded to mean all offshore accounts receive identical treatment, nor can existing accounts be assumed automatically exempt. The CSRC's same-day Q&A explicitly listed Southbound Stock Connect, QDII and Cross-boundary Wealth Management Connect as lawful channels. Whether a particular account is affected by a measure still requires checking the facts of the institution, service and funds. [CSRC, Q&A on the rectification plan, 2026-05-22](https://www.csrc.gov.cn/csrc/c100028/c7634328/content.shtml)

#### Wealth Management Connect: Regional Eligibility, a Closed Loop and a Product Pool All Apply

The HKMA's bank Southbound Scheme page was revised on May 11, 2026: eligibility includes a Greater Bay Area identity or residency connection and mainland regulatory requirements, and dedicated accounts operate under closed-loop management. Products include qualifying funds, bonds and deposits, but exclude investment products listed and traded on HKEX. The individual net-remittance quota is RMB 3 million, allocated as RMB 1.5 million each when both bank and securities-firm channels are used. This is a fund-flow quota, not an asset-market-value ceiling or an appropriate risk budget. [HKMA, bank Southbound Scheme arrangements, revised 2026-05-11](https://www.hkma.gov.hk/eng/key-functions/international-financial-centre/wealth-management-connect/southbound-scheme/)

Participation by securities firms also does not mean a dedicated account becomes an unrestricted Hong Kong stock-trading account. The SFC topic page rechecked in this review was updated on January 22, 2026 and lists the foundational circular dated January 24, 2024 and supplementary arrangements dated November 13, 2025. Paragraphs 35-36 of the original appendix specify the scope of funds and bonds and exclude products listed and traded on HKEX. The 2025 supplement primarily improves customer communications and product-introduction arrangements; it did not provide a basis for finding that general stock trading had been opened. [SFC, Wealth Management Connect topic page](https://www.sfc.hk/en/Regulatory-functions/Intermediaries/Supervision/Cross-boundary-WMC); [Appendix 1 to the 2024 circular](https://apps.sfc.hk/edistributionWeb/api/circular/openAppendix?appendix=0&lang=EN&refNo=24EC6); [2025 supplementary circular](https://apps.sfc.hk/edistributionWeb/api/circular/list-content/circular/intermediaries/supervision/doc?lang=EN&refNo=25EC60)

Document dates are retained here to distinguish a current webpage check from the original issuance date of a rule. This chapter has not verified any individual's eligibility or any institution's entire saleable-product list, nor does it describe the Hong Kong materials above as a compilation of all 2026 implementing rules in mainland China, Hong Kong and Macao. Product pools, institutional arrangements and suitability outcomes still require confirmation against the full documents at the time services are actually provided.

#### Southbound Stock Connect and QDII: Having a Channel Does Not Mean Having Every Instrument

HKEX's March 2026 ETF Connect materials state that eligible ETFs exclude synthetic ETFs and leveraged and inverse products. Thus, "a particular inverse product exists in Hong Kong" and "that product can be bought through Southbound Stock Connect" are two different statements. This chapter has not verified every entry on the day's eligible list, and does not equate similar index names with identical product eligibility. [HKEX, ETF Connect information for issuers, 2026-03](https://www.hkex.com.hk/-/media/HKEX-Market/Products/Securities/Exchange-Traded-Products/Launch/Inclusion-of-ETFs-in-Stock-Connect-info-for-issuers.pdf)

QDII, by contrast, refers to institutions approved to raise funds domestically and manage offshore securities investments as portfolios; fundholders obtain rights under the relevant product contract. Investment scope, use of derivatives, subscription and redemption arrangements, and unit-trading methods still need contract-by-contract review. The label "QDII" does not imply uniform risk or tax treatment. [CSRC, Articles 2 and 3 of the Trial Measures for QDII Administration, issued in 2007](https://www.csrc.gov.cn/csrc/c106256/c1653919/content.shtml)

#### Foreign Exchange, Accounts and Tax Cannot Substitute for One Another

SAFE's business materials published in 2023 explain that the equivalent of USD 50,000 is an individual's annual facilitation quota, not an absolute monetary ceiling on all lawful foreign-exchange uses, and not automatic permission for offshore securities investment. Foreign-exchange purchases under the facilitation arrangement have purpose restrictions. This review retains that basic boundary without presenting the 2023 materials as new 2026 rules, and makes no legal conclusion about a particular individual's full capital-account eligibility. [State Administration of Foreign Exchange, personal foreign-exchange business explanation, page 16 of the 2023 materials](https://www.safe.gov.cn/shanxi/file/file/20230704/261d099ca05b479c82f28d0b5e16cd57.pdf)

Analytically, compliance in account opening, cross-border service provision and remittance purpose, and tax obligations are related checks that cannot substitute for one another. Filing taxes does not automatically remedy an inapplicable funding route, and owning offshore assets does not automatically determine a single tax rate. Because this research lacks information on personal tax residence, asset location, income character and tax credits, it provides no uniform after-tax return or personal filing judgment.

### II. QDII Prices Contain Two Layers of Risk: NAV Changes and Discount/Premium Changes

When a fund holding offshore assets trades on a domestic secondary market, its price does not simply follow the offshore index. Fund NAV incorporates the portfolio, fees, exchange rates and other factors, while the secondary-market price is additionally affected by subscription/redemption access, trading hours and supply and demand. China Southern Asset Management's S&P 500 ETF premium-risk notice on September 24, 2026 explicitly warned that the secondary-market price was significantly above indicative NAV and that trading suspensions might be used as warnings. This was an issuer disclosure at that time, not a real-time premium quote on this report's evidence-collection date. [China Southern Asset Management, secondary-market trading-price premium-risk notice, 2026-09-24](https://epaper.cs.com.cn/zgzqb/html/2026-09/24/nw.D110000zgzqb_20260924_3-B062.htm)

Writing a unit price as "NAV at a comparable time × (1 + premium rate)" gives the following under simplified assumptions excluding distributions, taxes, fees and transaction costs:

Price return＝(1＋NAV return)×(1＋ending premium rate)÷(1＋initial premium rate)−1.

Assume NAV falls from 100 to 50, the purchase premium is 30%, and the sale discount is 5%. The price then falls from 130 to 47.5, producing a price return of −63.46%, rather than simply adding −50%, −30% and −5%. This example illustrates structure only; it does not mean any particular fund will experience these changes. Conversely, a rising NAV may be offset by a narrowing premium, while a falling underlying market may temporarily be obscured by a widening premium.

Valuation times must also be aligned in research: dividing an intraday domestic price by stale NAV from the previous offshore trading day may produce a "premium" containing market and exchange-rate changes not yet reflected in the accounts. Indicative NAV also does not mean every holder can immediately redeem at that figure. If the NAV used already includes exchange-rate effects, the model must not deduct RMB appreciation or depreciation again. Restricted subscriptions may weaken arbitrage, but guarantee neither a widening premium nor its reversion on a particular trading day.

Research records should therefore retain separately the underlying-asset date, NAV date, domestic quote time, subscription/redemption status and supporting announcements. Without simultaneous data, the premium remains "to be verified"; an apparently cheap price label is not upgraded into an executable trading condition.

### III. Being Right on Direction Can Still Lose Money: Additional Variables in Five Instrument Types

| Instrument type | Additional requirements beyond the research judgment | Inference that cannot be made |
|---|---|---|
| Buying put options | Size and timing of the decline, implied volatility, spreads, exercise and settlement arrangements | A correct long-term bearish view means a finite-maturity contract must profit |
| Shorting stock | Continued stock-borrow availability, borrow fees, margin, dividend compensation and forced-closeout conditions | A high company valuation gives losses a natural ceiling |
| Index futures | Contract multiplier, mark-to-market cash, margin changes, basis and rolling | Initial margin is the maximum possible loss |
| Leveraged and inverse products | Daily objectives, reset path, expenses, trading and capacity constraints | Multi-month returns must equal the index's cumulative return multiplied by the multiple in the product name |
| Volatility-related products | Actual tracked underlying, term structure, rolling, issuer and termination terms | A rise in spot VIX makes every related product rise in the same proportion |

A put option compresses "the price may fall" into a stricter proposition: the price must fall within the contract's life and by enough to cover the premium and costs. The premium on a separately purchased option may be lost entirely; even if the underlying falls, the option may lose money because implied volatility falls or time passes. Expiry and exercise may also create new settlement obligations or underlying positions, so "a purchased option has limited loss" cannot be extended indefinitely to the entire account after exercise. [SEC, options investor education, updated 2026-07-16](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/general-resources/news-alerts/alerts-bulletins/investor-bulletins-63)

Shorting stock and buying a put have different risk structures. A short position's price loss theoretically has no fixed ceiling and also involves borrow costs, dividend compensation and margin requirements. Long-term overvaluation can coexist with further short-term gains. Futures margin is performance collateral, not the full purchase price; daily marking to market converts price moves into cash requirements. Being "ultimately right on direction" cannot replace the ability to meet payments along the way. [SEC, basic short-selling risk explanation](https://www.sec.gov/investor/pubs/regsho.htm); [CFTC, futures margin and marking to market](https://www.cftc.gov/LearnAndProtect/EducationCenter/economicpurpose)

No specific broker fees or margin figures are listed here because they vary with the contract, client, volatility and institutional policies, and no account-level verification was conducted in this review. More importantly, research scenarios typically operate over longer horizons than instruments' funding and risk-management cycles. Expressing a long-term view with a tool requiring continual cash replenishment introduces another question the original research did not answer.

#### Daily Inverse Does Not Mean Inverse Over the Whole Period

Ignoring expenses, tracking error and rebalancing constraints, if an underlying rises 10% on the first day and falls 9.0909% on the second, it goes from 100 back to 100. An ideal daily −2x product instead falls from 100 to 80 and then rises to approximately 94.55, returning approximately −5.45% over the two days. This comes from the daily compounding path; it cannot be calculated by multiplying the underlying's two-day total return of 0 by −2.

This example also does not prove that a product decays at a fixed rate every day: persistent one-way trends, oscillation and the order of gains and losses can produce different results. There is no universal "annual decay of 15%-30%" for all inverse products, still less a long-term insurance cost that can be inferred from structural education.

Flexible-leverage structures appearing in 2026 add another constraint. Hong Kong's Investor and Financial Education Council explained on August 3 that some products' target multiples may be lowered daily and are not fixed each day at the upper limit shown in their names; the next day's target must be disclosed by the issuer. This research therefore does not carry forward a fixed-multiple list as representative of all current products, nor conflate HKEX listing eligibility with Southbound Stock Connect eligibility. [Hong Kong IFEC, flexible leverage structure, 2026-08-03](https://www.ifec.org.hk/web/en/investment/investment-products/leveraged-and-inverse-products/flexible-leverage-structure.page)

#### Volatility Exposure and Downside Insurance Are Not Synonyms

Many volatility exchange-traded products track VIX futures rather than spot VIX, and term structure and rolling affect holding results; ETNs also require checking issuer credit and other terms. Inferring "holding for a year must lose 60%-90%" from "prolonged market calm hurts some products," or "any VIX product provides long-term insurance" from "volatility rises during stock-market panic," goes beyond product-specific evidence. [FINRA, risks of volatility-linked exchange-traded products, 2017-10-26](https://syndication.finra.org/content/know-you-invest-volatility-linked-exchange-traded-products)

Common non-principal-protected snowball products tie coupons to knock-in, knock-out and other barrier conditions; economically, the buyer may resemble a seller of an exotic put, taking on rather than removing some downside risk. Different structures may include limited losses, protection clauses or different observation arrangements, so generalization is inappropriate. The coupon is not deposit interest, still less insurance that automatically pays out when AI assets fall. [CSRC Guangdong bureau, key trading elements of snowball products, 2021-08-24](https://www.csrc.gov.cn/guangdong/c105589/c3fc6e55bf3e64605959a103db73ad5b0/content.shtml)

### IV. Cross-Asset Buffers Must Be Judged by the Source of the Shock

"Technology stocks fall" is insufficient to establish the direction of gold, long-duration bonds, the US dollar or A-shares. If the shock comes from rising real interest rates, discounting pressure on duration assets may increase simultaneously; if it comes from falling demand with easing inflation constraints, the rate path may differ; if it comes from a liquidity squeeze, assets previously treated as buffers may also be sold. These are conditional relationships requiring separate estimation, not fixed correlations, still less a permanent hedging table.

Gold provides no contractual payment opposite to AI-company cash flow; bond prices depend on duration, credit and interest rates; foreign-currency assets also connect investment outcomes to domestic-currency liabilities. Chinese assets differ from US technology companies in business, policy and valuation foundations, but may still interact through exports, supply chains, risk appetite and global funding channels. Industry demand, share prices and exchange-rate changes require separate arguments. "Different regions" does not imply "no transmission."

For a portfolio, the real questions are which risk factor causes the original loss, whether a new instrument reduces that factor, and whether it instead adds new maturity, leverage, credit or liquidity risk. Without all holdings and liabilities, a purported net hedge effect cannot be calculated. This chapter therefore does not convert conditional analysis into personal gold, cash or cross-market allocation percentages.

### V. A Further Gate Remains Between Research and Implementation

Before a research view enters an actual decision, at least five mutually consistent records are needed: the applicable channel and identity basis; the specific product's latest contract and risk documents; funding arrangements matched to liability currencies; profit/loss and cash requirements including the worst path; and evidence conditions that would overturn the original judgment. These are not a purchase list, but boundaries preventing "understanding the story" from being mistaken for "having an executable plan."

This verification established that the channels are not interchangeable, identified structural risks in several products, and confirmed the dates of relevant official documents. It did not establish any individual's eligibility to open accounts, buy foreign exchange or trade, or their suitability; did not reread live prices, full product lists, fees or tax treatment; and executed no trades. Industry-risk research remains research at this point. Only additional complete facts and explicit authorization could support a separate discussion of implementation.

## Appendix C: The Shale-Oil Cycle Analogy - Technology Diffusion, Capital Constraints and Divergent Returns

### I. Historical Evidence on Technology Diffusion and Capital Returns

#### What This History Can Answer

The useful reference offered by shale oil is that technological progress, capital investment and capital returns may move at different speeds: greater productive capacity can change industry supply and competition without ensuring commensurate returns for every round of equity, debt or equipment investment. This does not mean shale oil and AI are "identical in structure," still less that AI must enter a downturn on the same timetable.

Research should ask who captures value after technology improves - consumers, equipment suppliers, asset owners or financiers; whose costs can fall alongside prices; and who must stop reinvesting when an industry develops a cash shortfall. Only when grounded in these constraints is a historical analogy more useful than saying "the technology is real, but there will be a bubble."

#### From Funding Gaps to Consolidation: Several Verifiable Milestones

| Period or date | Verified fact | Significance for this analogy |
|---|---|---|
| 2012 to early 2013 | In the EIA's sample of US onshore crude-oil producers, operating cash flow was approximately half of capital expenditure | Technological expansion needed external funding; this does not mean all companies were unable to recover their investment at the time |
